The principle of speech, which is the root of all the activities of the world, is manifested by Prajāpati and stands as the support of the world in the very form of Prajāpati. (This matter will be explained in the Special Topics of the Ṛk Ṛ. Saṃ. 10-81-7.) Viśvakarman, who is praised as the lord of speech, has also created the world on the basis of speech, and he is also of the form of Agni.

अग्निर्वा एषोऽग्नेरध्यजायत । शोकात्पृथिव्या उत वा दिवस्परीति यद्वै प्रजापतेः शोकादजायत तद्दिवश्च पृथिव्यै च शोकादजायत येन प्रजा विश्वकर्मा जजानेति वाग्वा अजो वाचो वै प्रजा विश्वकर्मा जजान तमग्ने हेडः परि ते वृणक्तु ॥ (*agnirvā eṣo 'gneradhyajāyata | śokātpṛthivyā uta vā divasparīti yadvai prajāpateḥ śokādajāyata taddivaśca pṛthivyai ca śokādajāyata yena prajā viśvakarmā jajāneti vāgvā ajo vāco vai prajā viśvakarmā jajāna tamagne heḍaḥ pari te vṛṇaktu ‖*) (Śa. Brā. 7-5-2-21)

Agni is one born of Agni. Everything that is produced as the result of Prajāpati's austerity has to be said to be produced as the result of the sorrow (austerity) of heaven and earth. Therefore Agni was born as the result of the sorrow (austerity) of heaven and earth. This is the very basis of Viśvakarman's creation. By the basis of that principle of speech which is without birth,

### Printed p. 172

named Aja, Prajāpati in the form of Viśvakarman created the world. Here the root of creation is of the nature of Agni, and what has been created is of the nature of Agni. Everything is pervaded by the brilliance of Agni. Therefore it is explained that it is said "the birth of Agni from Agni".

विश्वकर्मा ऋषिरिति । वाग्वै विश्वकर्मर्षिर्वाचा हीदग्ं सर्वं कृतं तस्माद्वाग्विश्वकर्मर्षिः प्रजापतिगृहीतया त्वयेति प्रजापतिसृष्टया त्वयेत्येतद्वाचं गृह्णामि प्रजाभ्य इति........ (*viśvakarmā ṛṣiriti | vāgvai viśvakarmarṣirvācā hīdagṃ sarvaṃ kṛtaṃ tasmādvāgviśvakarmarṣiḥ prajāpatigṛhītayā tvayeti prajāpatisṛṣṭayā tvayetyetadvācaṃ gṛhṇāmi prajābhya iti........*) (Śa. Bhā. 8-1-2-9) (Śa. Brā. 8-1-2-9) **NOTE**

Viśvakarman, the maker of everything, is himself the seer. Because speech is the root of all the activity of all created beings, Viśvakarman is of the form of speech. "To you, created by Prajāpati in the form of Viśvakarman, and to your offspring, I provide speech": in contexts where such descriptions occur, speech and Viśvakarman are identical. It was said above that the world was made by Viśvakarman on the basis of this speech. The sacrifice is the basis of the creation of the world made by him; the dissolution too is in the form of sacrifice. Therefore Viśvakarman is present in the form of the sacrificial fire. There is no sacrifice without husband and wife together. Therefore Agni and his wife together take part in this sacrificial act. How? Thus:

अथाहात्पात्नवतस्य यजेति । वृषा वा अग्नीद्योषा पत्नि मिथुनमेवैतत्पजननं क्रियते स जुहोत्यग्ना ३ पत्निवन्निति वृषा वा अग्निर्योषा पत्नि मिथुनमेवैतत्प जननं कुरुते ।........एतदंततो यज्ञस्य मिथुनात्पजननात्प जनयति ॥ (*athāhātpātnavatasya yajeti | vṛṣā vā agnīdyoṣā patni mithunamevaitatpajananaṃ kriyate sa juhotyagnā 3 patnivanniti vṛṣā vā agniryoṣā patni mithunamevaitatpa jananaṃ kurute |........etadaṃtato yajñasya mithunātpajananātpa janayati ‖*) (Śa. Brā. 4-4-2-8)

The rule is: offer the sacrificial oblation addressed to him who has a wife. The principle named Āgnīdha is the male form; the wife is the female form. From the union of this husband and wife comes the birth of offspring. One must offer the oblation addressing "O Agni who has a wife". Agni is the male; the wife is the female (the sentence अग्ने वाक् पत्नि (*agne vāk patni*) stands in this same context in the Kāṇva recension of the Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa). In this way, from the union of Agni, in the form of the husband, with the female in the form of the wife (speech), the birth of offspring is described. This Agni, the sacrificial person, is the one called Viśvakarman. The Agni whom it names as lord of speech, and by whose sacrificial act it says the world was produced, that Agni is of the form of Viśvakarman. This matter has been stated precisely in this way:

प्रत्येत्य वैश्वकर्माणि जुहोति । विश्वानि कर्माण्ययमग्निस्तान्यस्मात्र सर्वाणि कर्माणि कृतानि भवंति तान्येतत्प्रीणाति तानि हविषा देवतां (*pratyetya vaiśvakarmāṇi juhoti | viśvāni karmāṇyayamagnistānyasmātra sarvāṇi karmāṇi kṛtāni bhavaṃti tānyetatprīṇāti tāni haviṣā devatāṃ*)

### Printed p. 173

करोति ...... .... .... सा देवता न सा यस्यै न गृह्यतेऽथो विश्वकर्माय मग्निस्तमेवैतत्प्रीणाति ॥ (*karoti ...... .... .... sā devatā na sā yasyai na gṛhyate 'tho viśvakarmāya magnistamevaitatprīṇāti ‖*) (Śa. Brā. 9-5-1-42)

The fire-altar (agnicayana) prescribed here includes all rites. Because it includes all rites, there is here the rule of offering the oblation to Viśvakarman, since Agni is Viśvakarman. An oblation offered in Agni is as if offered to all the deities.

यद्दत्तं यत्परादानं । यत्पूर्तं याश्च दक्षिणाः । तदग्निर्वैश्वकर्मणः स्वर्देवेषु नो दधदिति यच्चैव संप्रति दद्यो यच्चासंप्रति तन्नोऽयमग्निर्वैश्वकर्मणः स्वर्गे लोके दधात्वित्येतत् ॥ (*yaddattaṃ yatparādānaṃ | yatpūrtaṃ yāśca dakṣiṇāḥ | tadagnirvaiśvakarmaṇaḥ svardeveṣu no dadhaditi yaccaiva saṃprati dadyo yaccāsaṃprati tanno 'yamagnirvaiśvakarmaṇaḥ svarge loke dadhātvityetat ‖*) (Śa. Batra, 9-5-1-49) **NOTE**

Whatever gift is given by us addressed to Agni, whatever resolve is fulfilled, whatever oblations and the like are offered, all that Agni in the form of Viśvakarman establishes among the gods. Whatever we offer, whether in the prescribed seasons or at other times, all that he establishes in the world of the gods.

यत्र धारा अनपेताः । मधोर्घृतस्य च याः तदग्निर्वैश्वकर्मणः स्वर्देवेषु नो दधदिति यथैव यजस्तथा बंधुः ॥ (*yatra dhārā anapetāḥ | madhorghṛtasya ca yāḥ tadagnirvaiśvakarmaṇaḥ svardeveṣu no dadhaditi yathaiva yajastathā baṃdhuḥ ‖*) (Śa. Cā. 9-5-1250) **NOTE**

In the world of the gods where, without interruption, uninterrupted streams of honey and streams of ghee flow, in that world may Agni in the form of Viśvakarman establish us. May he cause the fruit suited to our prescribed rite to be obtained. In this way, showing the identity of Agni and Viśvakarman, it is praised that every oblation offered in Agni (in the fire-altar) gives satisfaction to Agni in the form of Viśvakarman and gives its fruit.

The word Viśvakarman, according to the context, is an epithet praising the nature of Indra and of Āditya. It means the power that makes everything. The names Agni, Indra and Āditya are names suited to the respective world and the respective rite, and they do not show a difference in the root principle; this was stated earlier, when the three kinds of forms of Agni were described at the time of the praise of Agni. Therefore, if Agni is Viśvakarman, there is no impropriety in Indra and Āditya also being designated by the same name. Describing Indra by the same epithet, the Ṛk Saṃhitā says thus—

### Printed p. 174

त्वमिंद्राभिभूरसि त्वं सूर्यमरोचयः । विश्वकर्मा विश्वदेवो महा असि ॥ (*tvamiṃdrābhibhūrasi tvaṃ sūryamarocayaḥ | viśvakarmā viśvadevo mahā asi ‖*) (Ṛ. Saṃ. 89-2) **NOTE**

O Indra, you are the conqueror of all enemies. You have made Āditya radiant. Doing all works (you alone doing the work of all the deities) you are designated by the name of each deity. In this way, in the context of praising Indra's character as Viśvakarman, this epithet describes his greatness.

विभ्राजइत्योतिषा स्वंगरच्छो रोचनं दिवः । येनेमा विश्वा भुवनान्याभृता विश्वकर्मणा विश्वदेव्यावत ॥ (*vibhrājaityotiṣā svaṃgaraccho rocanaṃ divaḥ | yenemā viśvā bhuvanānyābhṛtā viśvakarmaṇā viśvadevyāvata ‖*) (Ṛ. Saṃ. 10-170-4)

O Āditya, making the whole world shine with your radiance, you have your place in the heaven-world. With the help of your radiances you are able to carry on all activities. For that very reason you are Viśvakarman. And you draw up with your radiance the water, the means of the sacrifice that benefits all the deities, and spread it over the world. For that very reason Āditya is praised as all-powerful, skilled in all works, and so on.

In this way, in the forms of Agni, Indra and Āditya, pervading the three worlds, one and the same principle, which is the cause, the support and the refuge of the whole world, is spoken of as Viśvakarman. Durgācārya, explaining this very matter in his commentary on the Nirukta, says thus.

सार्थिवाप्य धातू तेजसा परिपच्यमानौ वायुः व्यूहेन वि चरन् सर्वभावानुप्रवेशं सर्वमिदमत्यद्भुतमचिंत्यमकृतात्मभिर्जगत् करोतीति स मध्यमः । विश्वकरणाद्विश्वकर्मा । (*sārthivāpya dhātū tejasā paripacyamānau vāyuḥ vyūhena vi caran sarvabhāvānupraveśaṃ sarvamidamatyadbhutamaciṃtyamakṛtātmabhirjagat karotīti sa madhyamaḥ | viśvakaraṇādviśvakarmā |*) (Ni. Bhā. 10-25) **NOTE**

He divides the elements of earth and atmosphere, which have been ripened by radiance, spreads them out according to the occasion, enters into them himself, and creates the world, which is full of variety and wonderful. Because he creates everything by his own power, he has the name Viśvakarman. The Śruti passages also praise this very thing. For example—

आथैष वैश्वकर्मणो विश्वानि मे कर्माणि कृतान्यासन्निति विश्वकर्मा हि सोऽभवत्॥ (*āthaiṣa vaiśvakarmaṇo viśvāni me karmāṇi kṛtānyāsanniti viśvakarmā hi so 'bhavat‖*) (Mai. Saṃ. 1-10-16) **GLOSS**

Such explanations praise the Viśvakarman-nature of the primal principle.

### Printed p. 175

Viśvakarman, thus described, resolved to create the world and created the world by his own act in the form of a sacrifice. This matter is stated in some places in the form of a narrative.

तेन हौतेन विश्वकर्मा भौवन ईजे । तेनेष्ट्वात्यतिष्ठत्सर्वाणि भूतानीदग्ं सर्वमभवदतितिष्ठति सर्वाणि भूतानीदग्ं सर्वं भवति य एवं विद्वान्तर्वमेधेन यजते योवैतदेवं वेद ॥ (*tena hautena viśvakarmā bhauvana īje | teneṣṭvātyatiṣṭhatsarvāṇi bhūtānīdagṃ sarvamabhavadatitiṣṭhati sarvāṇi bhūtānīdagṃ sarvaṃ bhavati ya evaṃ vidvāntarvamedhena yajate yovaitadevaṃ veda ‖*) (Ka. Patra, 13-7-194) **NOTE**

Viśvakarman Bhauvana performed this Sarvamedha sacrifice. As its fruit he attained the position that surpasses all beings, and he himself took all forms. Whoever performs this Sarvamedha sacrifice, he too, by the grace of Viśvakarman, attains in the same way the position that surpasses all beings and obtains the forms of them all; so it is said.

In this context another passage is stated, but its purport cannot be given with certainty.

तग्ं ह कश्यपो याजयांचकार। तदपि भूमिः श्लोकं जगौ न मा मर्त्यः कश्चन दातुर्महति विश्वकर्मन् भौवन मंद आसिथ उपमंघ्यति स्या सलिलास्य मध्ये मृषैष ते संगः ते कश्यपायेति ॥ (*tagṃ ha kaśyapo yājayāṃcakāra| tadapi bhūmiḥ ślokaṃ jagau na mā martyaḥ kaścana dāturmahati viśvakarman bhauvana maṃda āsitha upamaṃghyati syā salilāsya madhye mṛṣaiṣa te saṃgaḥ te kaśyapāyeti ‖*) (Pa. Brā. 13-7-1-15) **NOTE**

In this sacrifice Kaśyapa helped Viśvakarman. Then the Earth speaks this word: O Viśvakarman Bhauvana, no mortal is able to give me away as a gift. This resolve of yours shows your presumption. This Earth sinks into the midst of the waters. The promise you have made to Kaśyapa is vain and will bear no fruit. The occasion for saying this is not so clear in the original passages. Therefore Sāyaṇa has made it clear in his commentary.

अस्मै च विश्वकर्मा भूमिं दातुमियेष। तत्र च काले भूमिरपीमं श्लोकं गीतवती ॥ (*asmai ca viśvakarmā bhūmiṃ dātumiyeṣa| tatra ca kāle bhūmirapīmaṃ ślokaṃ gītavatī ‖*) (Śa. Brā. 13-7-1-15)

Viśvakarman wished to give the Earth itself as a gift to Kaśyapa, who had helped him in his sacrificial work. Then the Earth, not tolerating it, spoke to Viśvakarman in this way. Thus Sāyaṇa has given the occasion for this speech of the Earth. The same matter is also told in the Aitareya Brāhmaṇa:

### Printed p. 176

एतेन ह वा ऐंद्रेण महाभिषेकेण कश्यपो विश्वकर्माणं भौवनमभिषिषेच तस्मादु विश्वकर्माभौवनः समंतं सर्वतः पृथिवीं जयन् परीयायाश्वेन च मेध्येनेजे भूमिर्ह जगावित्युदाहरंति न मा मर्त्यः कश्चन सातुमर्हति................। (*etena ha vā aiṃdreṇa mahābhiṣekeṇa kaśyapo viśvakarmāṇaṃ bhauvanamabhiṣiṣeca tasmādu viśvakarmābhauvanaḥ samaṃtaṃ sarvataḥ pṛthivīṃ jayan parīyāyāśvena ca medhyeneje bhūmirha jagāvityudāharaṃti na mā martyaḥ kaścana sātumarhati................|*) (Ai. Brā. 8-21)

By the Aindra Mahābhiṣeka praised above (Ai. Brā. 8-21) Kaśyapa consecrated Viśvakarman Bhauvana. As its result Viśvakarman Bhauvana attained universal sovereignty, travelled over and conquered the whole Earth, and performed the horse sacrifice. Then the Earth (addressing Viśvakarman, who was giving her away to Kaśyapa) said: "O Viśvakarman, no one can give me away", and so on. Thus this matter is told in a somewhat different way.

विश्वकर्मा ऋषिरिति । वाग्वै विश्वकर्मा ऋषिरि......... ॥ (*viśvakarmā ṛṣiriti | vāgvai viśvakarmā ṛṣiri......... ‖*) (Śa. Brā. 8-1-2-9)

In this passage quoted above, and in other contexts also, Viśvakarman himself is said to be a seer. The word ṛṣi here means the all-knowing. The divine Viśvakarman, who is all-knowing, all-powerful, all-pervading and skilled in all works, is the seer Viśvakarman. The seer who composed the present sūktas (10-81, 82) is also a follower of Viśvakarman **NOTE**. According to the doctrine of this great seer, what the world is can here be examined in detail.

According to the doctrine of this great seer, water is the original support of the whole world: तमिद्दर्भंप्रथमं दध्र आपः ॥ (*tamiddarbhaṃprathamaṃ dadhra āpaḥ ‖*) (10-82-6). But since this water too is a produced thing, the question what its source is arises naturally. For that very reason there is also the question कंस्विद्धर्भं प्रथमं दध्र आपः (*kaṃsviddharbhaṃ prathamaṃ dadhra āpaḥ*) (10-82-5). The first cause of the whole world is Īśvara. He is described by such epithets as विश्वतश्चक्षुः (*viśvataścakṣuḥ*). He alone is the god: जनयन् देव एकः (*janayan deva ekaḥ*); he is the producer of everything. योनः पिता (*yonaḥ pitā*): he is the refuge of all the deities: यत्र देवाः समपश्यंत विश्वे (*yatra devāḥ samapaśyaṃta viśve*).

Thus Īśvara, who is all-knowing, all-powerful and all-pervading, cannot be seen with the worldly eye: न तं विदाथ य इमा जजानान्यद्युष्माकमंतरु बभूव (*na taṃ vidātha ya imā jajānānyadyuṣmākamaṃtaru babhūva*). His form is different from the other forms of the world. Covered by ignorance, we cannot see him. Yet the sight of him is utterly necessary.

### Printed p. 177

Both the power of performing the sacrifice and the power of creation are united in this Viśvakarman. The harmony of these two powers united in Agni has been examined earlier. In the same way these elements are present also in Hiraṇyagarbha, another form of Āditya, as will be explained later in the Hiraṇyagarbha sūkta. The same powers that are in harmony in Agni and in Hiraṇyagarbha are in harmony also in Viśvakarman. The element of performing the sacrifice, स्वयं यजस्व तन्वं (*svayaṃ yajasva tanvaṃ*) (10-81-5), alone shows the distinctive character of Agni. Epithets such as विश्वतश्चक्षुः (*viśvataścakṣuḥ*) show the distinctive character of Āditya. Ṛks 5 and 6 of sūkta 82 state the matters explained in Ṛks 7 and 8 of the Hiraṇyagarbha sūkta. Both these contexts too praise the primal principle in the form of Āditya.

The same kind of explanation is found also in the Taittirīya Saṃhitā. The main points are all as in the Ṛk Saṃhitā; but only the explanation that is not in the Ṛk Saṃhitā is quoted and explained here.

विश्वकर्माह्यजनिष्ट देव आदिद्दंधर्वो अभवद्वितीयः । तृतीयः पिता जनितौषधीनामपां गर्भं व्यदधात् पुरुत्रा ॥ (*viśvakarmāhyajaniṣṭa deva ādiddaṃdharvo abhavadvitīyaḥ | tṛtīyaḥ pitā janitauṣadhīnāmapāṃ garbhaṃ vyadadhāt purutrā ‖*) (Tai. Saṃ. 4-6-2-3)

The god Viśvakarman was born first. Second the Gandharva, and third the father, the producer of the plants, were born. And in many places the germ in the form of water was established. This account tells of the birth of Viśvakarman. Moreover,

विश्वकर्मन् हविषा वर्धनेन त्रातारमिंद्रमकृणोरवध्यं । तस्मै विशः समनमंत पूर्वीरयमुग्रो विहव्यो यथासत् ॥ (*viśvakarman haviṣā vardhanena trātāramiṃdramakṛṇoravadhyaṃ | tasmai viśaḥ samanamaṃta pūrvīrayamugro vihavyo yathāsat ‖*) (Tai. Saṃ. 4-6-3)

O Viśvakarman, being inspired by the strength-giving oblation, you saved Indra, the protector of the world, from harm. All the ancient seers offered him their devout salutations. Everyone showed him devotion and made him worthy of invocation. All that is your power: this shows the exceeding power of Viśvakarman. For that very reason,

समुद्राय वयुनाय सिंधूनां पतये नमः । नदीनाग्ं सर्वासां पित्रे जुहुता विश्वकर्मणे विश्वाहामर्त्यग्ं हविः ॥ (*samudrāya vayunāya siṃdhūnāṃ pataye namaḥ | nadīnāgṃ sarvāsāṃ pitre juhutā viśvakarmaṇe viśvāhāmartyagṃ haviḥ ‖*) (Tai. Saṃ. 4-6-2-6)

### Printed p. 178

Salutation to the ocean, the lord of the rivers and of movement; make a constant offering to Viśvakarman, the lord of the rivers; thus worship befitting the greatness of Viśvakarman is enjoined. Certain special features of Viśvakarman that are not described in the Ṛk Saṃhitā are known from these passages recited in the Taittirīya Saṃhitā.

Prajāpati, in the form of Viśvakarman, withdraws the universe in the form of a sacrifice; desires to extend again the universe offered into himself in the form of an oblation; and pervades together with all the things of the extended universe. These matters are described in the first Ṛk (10-81-1). To examine these points first is the matter in hand. These matters will be explained in the special topics of the first Ṛk.

Some deities are of the nature of conceptions, that is, the term deity is used for certain conceptions of the mind. All these actions belong to the one supreme god. Viśvakarman is one of such names. In the tenth maṇḍala alone this name occurs only five times, and there are two sūktas praising this deity (10-81; 10-82). The same word Viśvakarman is used once as an epithet of Indra (4-87-2) and once (10-170-4) as an epithet of Sūra, who creates everything. In the other Vedas its use as an epithet is common. It is an epithet of Prajāpati also (Vā. Saṃ. 12-61). In the two sūktas of the Ṛgveda Viśvakarman is described in this way. He is all-seeing; he has eyes on all sides, one face, arms and feet. According to this description Viśvakarman resembles Brahmā, who has four faces and four arms. He has wings also. He is a seer, a priest and a father. He is lord of speech; swift as thought; benevolent; the source of every kind of prosperity. All regions and all beings are known to him; he it is who tells all the deities their names. Wise and able; the supreme among superhuman persons. The support of everything, and terrible; he created the earth and brought the sky into light. Probably this word was at first used mainly as an epithet of Sūra, and later came to be regarded as a separate deity. Architecture is the special feature of this deity. In the Brāhmaṇas Viśvakarman and Prajāpati are clearly said to be one (compare Śa. Brā. 8-2-1-10; 8-2-2-13; Ai. Brā. 4-22). In the Purāṇas and elsewhere there are accounts that Viśvakarman is the carpenter of the gods.

##### ॥ सायणभाष्यम् ॥ (Sāyaṇa-bhāṣya)

> य इमेति सप्तर्चं त्रयोदशं सूक्तं भुवनपुत्रस्य विश्वकर्मण आर्षं
> त्रैष्टुभं विश्वकर्मदैवत्यं । कथा चानुक्रांतं । य इमा विश्वकर्मा भौवनो

### Printed p. 179

> वैश्वकर्मणं त्विति ॥ गतः सूक्तविनियोगः ॥ अत्र वाजसनेयकं । ब्रह्मा वै स्वयंभूस्तपोऽतप्यत । तदैक्षत न वै तपस्यानंत्यमस्ति हंताहं भूतेष्वात्मानं जुहवानि
> भूतानि चात्मनीति तत्सर्वेषु भूतेष्वात्मानं हुत्वेत्यादि । शत. १३-७-१ ॥

##### Gist of the bhāṣya

The sūkta beginning य इम (*ya ima*) has seven Ṛks and is the thirteenth sūkta. Its seer is Viśvakarman, the son of Bhuvana; its metre is Triṣṭubh; its deity is Viśvakarman. The Anukramaṇī records it thus: य इमा विश्वकर्मा भौवनो वैश्वकर्मणं त्विति ॥ (*ya imā viśvakarmā bhauvano vaiśvakarmaṇaṃ tviti ‖*). The use of the sūkta has been stated. Here is the Vājasaneyaka passage: Brahmā Svayambhū practised austerity. He reflected: there is no endlessness in austerity; let me offer myself in the beings and the beings in myself. Having offered himself in all beings, and so on (Śat. 13-7-1).

##### Anuvāda

This sūkta beginning य इमा (*ya imā*) is the thirteenth sūkta in the sixth anuvāka. It has seven Ṛks. The seer of this sūkta is Viśvakarman, son of Bhuvana; Viśvakarman is the deity; Triṣṭubh is the metre. In the Anukramaṇī it is said य इमा भौवनो वैश्व कर्मणं तु (*ya imā bhauvano vaiśva karmaṇaṃ tu*) **NOTE**. The use of the sūkta has been stated before. In the Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa of the Vājasaneya Śruti it is said: ब्रह्म वै स्वयंभु तपोऽतप्यत । तदैक्षत न वै तपस्यानंत्य. मस्ति हंताहं भूतेष्वात्मानं जुहवानि भूतानि चात्मनीति तत्सर्वेषु भूतेष्वात्मानं हुत्वा (*brahma vai svayaṃbhu tapo 'tapyata | tadaikṣata na vai tapasyānaṃtya. masti haṃtāhaṃ bhūteṣvātmānaṃ juhavāni bhūtāni cātmanīti tatsarveṣu bhūteṣvātmānaṃ hutvā*) (Śa. Brā. 13-7-1), and so on.

Maṇḍala 10; Anuvāka 6; Sūkta 81. Aṣṭaka 8; Adhyāya 3; Varga 16. Number of Ṛks in the sūkta: 7. Seer: विश्वकर्मा भौवनः (*viśvakarmā bhauvanaḥ*). Deity: विश्वकर्मा (*viśvakarmā*). Metre: त्रिष्टुप् (*triṣṭup*).

#### Ṛk 1

##### ॥ संहितापाठः ॥ (Saṃhitā-pāṭha)

> य इ॒मा विश्वा॒ भुव॑नानि॒ जुह्व॒दृषि॒र्होता॒ न्यसी॑दत्पि॒ता नः॑ ।  
> स आ॒शिषा॒ द्रवि॑णमि॒च्छमा॑नः प्रथम॒च्छदव॑रा॒ आ वि॑वेश ॥ १ ॥

##### ॥ पदपाठः ॥ (Pada-pāṭha)

> यः । इमा । विश्वा । भुवनानि । जुह्वत् । ऋषिः । होता । नि । असीदत् । पिता । नः ।  
> सः । आशिषा । द्रविणं । इच्छमानः । प्रथमछत् । अवरान् । आ । विवेश ॥ १ ॥

##### ॥ सायणभाष्यम् ॥ (Sāyaṇa-bhāṣya)

> अत्र निरुक्तं । विश्वकर्मा सर्वमेधे सर्वाणि भूतानि जुहवांचकार
> स आत्मानमप्यंततो जुहवांचकार । तदभिवादिन्येषर्घर्वति य इमा विश्वा भुवनानि

##### Gist of the bhāṣya

Here the Nirukta says: Viśvakarman at the Sarvamedha offered all beings, and in the end he offered himself also. About this the following Ṛk speaks: य इमा विश्वा भुवनानि (*ya imā viśvā bhuvanāni*) (the quotation breaks off at the end of the window).

### Printed p. 180

> जुह्वदिति । नि. १०-२६ । इति ॥ यो विश्वकर्मैतन्नामक ऋषिभुवनपुत्रो होता
> होमनिष्पादकः सन्विश्वा सर्वाणि भुवनानि जुह्वद्दोमं कुर्वन् । प्रथमं सर्वं
> जगद्भ्येत्यर्थः । पश्चादग्नौ नृसीदत् पिता जनकः । आत्मकृतेन कर्मणा
> देहोत्पत्तेः । न चैकस्य जन्यजनकभावो विरुध्यते तपोबलेन शरीरद्वयस्वीकारात् ।
> स एकधा भवति । छां. उ. ७.२६-२ । इत्यादिश्रुतेः । स ऋषिराशिषाशिष्टतिपादकेन
> सूक्त वाकादिना द्रविणं धनं स्वर्गाख्यमिच्छमानः कामयमानः प्रथमच्छत्पथममग्नेर्भुवनैराच्छादयितावरान्विप्रकृष्णान्भूतान्सात्मनाहुताना विवेश । आविष्टवानग्नि मित्युक्तार्थ एव पुनर्विशेषेणोक्तः । एवमुत्तरत्राधियज्ञ परतया योज्यं॥
> अथाध्यात्मप्रसिद्धोच्यते । यो विश्वकर्मा परमेश्वर इमा विश्वा भुवनानि
> जुह्वत्पलयकाले पृथिव्यानीनिमान्सप्त लोकान्नात्मन्याहुतिप्रक्षेपवत्संहरन्नृषिरतींद्रियद्रष्टा सर्वज्ञो होता संहाररूपस्य होमस्य कर्ता नोऽस्माकं
> पिता जनको निषसाद स्वयं स्थितवान् । आयमर्थः । प्रलयकाले प्राप्ते सति
> सर्वाल्लोकान्संहृत्यास्माकमपि संहर्ता पुनः सृष्टा च सन् सर्वज्ञो यः
> परमेश्वरः स्वयमेक एवासीत् । तथा हि श्रुतयः । आत्मा ना इदमेक एवाग्र
> आसीत् । ऐ. अ. २-४-१ । सदेव सोम्येदमग्र आसीत् । छां. उ. ६-२-१ । इत्यादिकाः ।
> स तादृशः परमेश्वर आशिषा बहुः स्यां प्रजायेयेत्येवंरूपया पुनःपुनः
> सिसृष्टया द्रविणमिच्छमानः धनोपलक्षितं जगद्भोगमाकांक्षमाणः प्रथमच्छत्पथमं मुख्यं निष्प्रपंचं पारमार्थिकं रूपमावृण्णन्नवरान्सृष्टान्साणिहृदयप्रदेशाना विवेश । आविष्टवाइवरूपेण । तथा च श्रूयते । सोऽकाम.
> यत बहुः स्यां प्रजायेयेति स तपोऽतप्यत स तपस्तप्तदं सर्वमसृजत
> यदिदं किंच तत्सृष्ट्वा तदेवानुप्राविशत् । तै. आ. ८-६ । इति । एवमन्या अप्युपनिषद
> उदाहार्याः ॥

##### Gist of the bhāṣya

The word जुह्वदिति (*juhvaditi*) is cited with the reference (Ni. 10-26). First the sacrificial explanation (adhiyajña). The seer named Viśvakarman, son of Bhuvana, being the Hotṛ, the accomplisher of the offering, makes the offering of all the worlds (जुह्वत् (*juhvat*)): first he offers the whole world; afterwards he sat down in Agni. **NOTE** "Father" means begetter, because the body arises through his own deed. There is no contradiction in one person being both begotten and begetter, since by the power of austerity he takes two bodies; for the Śruti says स एकधा भवति (*sa ekadhā bhavati*) (Chāṃ. U. 7.26-2) **NOTE**, and so on. That seer, desiring by blessing, that is by a sūkta-utterance and the like that conveys the blessing, wealth named heaven, covered Agni first with the worlds (प्रथमच्छत् (*prathamacchat*)) and entered the lower, remote beings offered by himself (अवरान् (*avarān*) आ विवेश (*ā viveśa*)): the meaning already stated, that he entered Agni, is stated again with a specification. In the same way the later Ṛks are to be applied with reference to the sacrifice.

Now the explanation with reference to the self (adhyātma) is given. Viśvakarman, the supreme Lord, at the time of dissolution withdraws into himself these worlds, the seven beginning with the earth, as one casts in an oblation. He is the seer, who sees beyond the senses, the omniscient, the Hotṛ, the doer of the offering in the form of dissolution; he is our father, and he sat down, that is, remained by himself. The sense is this: when the time of dissolution comes, he withdraws all the worlds, is the destroyer of us also, and then again the creator, omniscient, the supreme Lord alone was. So the Śrutis say: आत्मा ना इदमेक एवाग्र आसीत् । (*ātmā nā idameka evāgra āsīt |*) (Ai. A. 2-4-1) **NOTE**; सदेव सोम्येदमग्र आसीत् (*sadeva somyedamagra āsīt*) (Chāṃ. U. 6-2-1), and others. That same supreme Lord, by the blessing of the form "may I be many, may I be born", desiring wealth, that is, desiring the enjoyment of the world marked by wealth, in his wish to create again and again, covering first the principal, world-free, real form, entered the lower, created beings, the heart-regions of the embodied, as if entered, in appearance. And so it is heard: सोऽकाम. यत बहुः स्यां प्रजायेयेति स तपोऽतप्यत स तपस्तप्तदं सर्वमसृजत यदिदं किंच तत्सृष्ट्वा तदेवानुप्राविशत् । (*so 'kāma. yata bahuḥ syāṃ prajāyeyeti sa tapo 'tapyata sa tapastaptadaṃ sarvamasṛjata yadidaṃ kiṃca tatsṛṣṭvā tadevānuprāviśat |*) (Tai. Ā. 8-6) **NOTE**. Other Upaniṣad passages too may be cited in the same way.

##### प्रतिपदार्थ (Pratipadārtha)

- ऋषि (*ṛṣi*)—the all-knowing
- होता (*hotā*)—the accomplisher of the offering
- यः (*yaḥ*)—which Viśvakarman
- विश्वा (*viśvā*)—all
- इमा भुवनानि (*imā bhuvanāni*)—these worlds
- जुह्वत् (*juhvat*)—having offered in the form of an oblation
- न्यसीदत् (*nyasīdat*)—sat in his divine place
- सः (*saḥ*)—such
- नः पिता (*naḥ pitā*)—the same Viśvakarman, the begetter of us all
- आशिषा (*āśiṣā*)—accompanied by blessing
- द्रविणं (*draviṇaṃ*)—wealth
- इच्छमानः (*icchamānaḥ*)—desiring
- प्रथमच्छत् (*prathamacchat*)—having pervaded everything first in the form of Agni
- अवरान् (*avarān*)—all the beings offered by himself
- आ विवेश (*ā viveśa*)—entered

### Printed p. 181

##### भावार्थ (Bhāvārtha)

The same Viśvakarman, who is all-knowing and the accomplisher of the offering, who offered all the worlds in the form of an oblation and sat in his divine place, and who is the begetter of us all, desired wealth accompanied by blessing, pervaded everything first in the form of Agni, and entered all the beings offered by himself.

##### English translation
The Rishi, the Hotri, our father who offering all these worlds has taken has seat ; desiring wealth by pious benedictions, the first inventor has entered inferior (beings).

##### विशेषविषयगळु (Special Topics)

In this Ṛk four chief acts of Viśvakarman are described. The first of them:

1) यः इमा विश्वा भुवनानि जुह्वत् (*yaḥ imā viśvā bhuvanāni juhvat*): the matter that Viśvakarman offered this whole universe in the form of a sacrificial oblation. The second:

2) स आशिषा द्रविणमिच्छमानः (*sa āśiṣā draviṇamicchamānaḥ*): the desire to create again and again this very world that was offered as an oblation. The third:

3) प्रथमच्छत् (*prathamacchat*): the possession of a form that is free from the manifold world and is real in the highest sense. And last:

4) अवरान् आ विवेशः (*avarān ā viveśaḥ*): entering the hearts of all the beings created by his own will and being the inner controller of all. Thus the acts of creation, preservation and dissolution are described. Here another matter deserves attention. Among the acts of creation and the rest, creation is not described first. First the act in the form of dissolution; afterwards the wish to create, then creation; at last his pervading of the created world. In this order the acts of Viśvakarman are praised.

To withdraw the created world into himself at the time of dissolution and to remain alone, without a second, is one of the sports of Īśvara. Though from the standpoint of the world this act is destruction, from the standpoint of Īśvara it is regarded as merely a particular state. This offering of an oblation in the form of dissolution is explained in many contexts. The author of the Nirukta, speaking of one such context,

### Printed p. 182

तत्रेतिहासमाचक्षते । विश्वकर्मा भौवनः सर्वमेधे सर्वाणि भूतानि जुहवांचकार । स आत्मानमप्यंततो जुहवांचकार । तदभिवादिन्येषर्गवति । य इमा विश्वा इति ॥ (*tatretihāsamācakṣate | viśvakarmā bhauvanaḥ sarvamedhe sarvāṇi bhūtāni juhavāṃcakāra | sa ātmānamapyaṃtato juhavāṃcakāra | tadabhivādinyeṣargavati | ya imā viśvā iti ‖*) (Ni. 10-26)

This context is told in the form of a narrative. Viśvakarman, the son of Bhuvana, offered all beings in the form of oblations at the Sarvamedha sacrifice. At the end he offered himself also in the form of an oblation. The mantra य इमा विश्वा (*ya imā viśvā*) (10-81-1) makes this clear, he says. This very act of offering is explained thus in the Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa.

ब्रह्म वै स्वयंभु तपोऽतप्यत । तदैक्षत न वै तपस्यानंतमस्ति हंताहं भूतेष्वात्मानं जुहवानि भूतानि चात्मनीति तत्सर्वेषु भूतेष्वात्मानग्ं हुत्वा भूतानि चात्मनि सर्वेषां भूतानाग् श्रैष्ठ्यग् स्वाराज्यमाधिपत्यं पर्येति ॥ (*brahma vai svayaṃbhu tapo 'tapyata | tadaikṣata na vai tapasyānaṃtamasti haṃtāhaṃ bhūteṣvātmānaṃ juhavāni bhūtāni cātmanīti tatsarveṣu bhūteṣvātmānagṃ hutvā bhūtāni cātmani sarveṣāṃ bhūtānāg śraiṣṭhyag svārājyamādhipatyaṃ paryeti ‖*) (Ka. Cā. 13-7-1-1) **NOTE**

तेनैतेन विश्वकर्मा भौवन ईजे । तेनेष्टात्यतिष्ठत्सर्वाणि भूतानीदग्ं सर्वमभवत् .... ..... ...... .... य एनं विद्वान् .... .... .... ॥ (*tenaitena viśvakarmā bhauvana īje | teneṣṭātyatiṣṭhatsarvāṇi bhūtānīdagṃ sarvamabhavat .... ..... ...... .... ya enaṃ vidvān .... .... .... ‖*) (Śa. Brā. 13-7-194)

Brahmā, named Svayambhū, was practising austerity. Then he thought thus: austerity should not be endless. In its place I shall perform the Sarvamedha. I shall offer myself in the beings in the form of an oblation, and I shall offer the beings in myself in the form of an oblation. By offering himself in the beings and all the beings in himself in this way, he obtained his own sovereignty and lordship over all the worlds.

Viśvakarman, the son of Bhuvana, also performed a Sarvamedha of this kind. As its fruit he obtained a position higher than all beings, and himself obtained the form of all beings; so it is stated. This kind of Sarvamedha is one of the acts of the Creator. He who is the creator is the destroyer. The world created by himself is the oblation to himself; it is an oblation by himself to himself. This is a kind of sacrifice. As the sacrifice is the first cause of the creation of the world, so the dissolution of the universe too is in the form of a sacrifice. The sacrifice that is praised as सर्वॆषां भूताना., मात्मा यज्ञः (*sarvèṣāṃ bhūtānā., mātmā yajñaḥ*) (Śa. Brā. 14-3-2-1) **NOTE**, that same sacrifice is also the devourer of all beings.

### Printed p. 183

यज्ञो वै भुज्युरज्ञो हि सर्वाणि भूतानि भुनक्ति (*yajño vai bhujyurajño hi sarvāṇi bhūtāni bhunakti*) (Śa. Brā. 9-4-1-11)

Because the sacrifice itself enjoys all beings, the name Bhujyu is well known for the sacrifice. It is also his nature to enjoy the very worlds that Prajāpati produces. Among produced things none can be eternal; each must meet dissolution. Prajāpati, the creator, is himself the dissolver.

एको हि रुद्रो न द्वितीयाय तस्थुर्य इमान् लोकानीशत ईशनीभिः । प्रत्यज् जनास्तिष्ठति संचुकोचांतकाले संसृज्य विश्वा भुवनानि गोपाः ॥ (*eko hi rudro na dvitīyāya tasthurya imān lokānīśata īśanībhiḥ | pratyaj janāstiṣṭhati saṃcukocāṃtakāle saṃsṛjya viśvā bhuvanāni gopāḥ ‖*) (Śve. U. 3.2) **NOTE**

The unique Rudra, who governs and protects the world, pervades the whole world and is the inner controller of all. Having spread out the world in its subtle form, as soon as he knows that the business of the world is finished, he draws the whole universe into himself. This is the dissolution. In the sentence अस्य वामस्य पलितस्य होतुः (*asya vāmasya palitasya hotuḥ*) (Ṛ. Saṃ. 1-164-1) the word होतुः (*hotuḥ*) is given the meaning संहर्तुः (*saṃhartuḥ*), and so this very act of dissolution is explained.

स यद्यदेवासृजत तत्तदत्तुमध्रियत सर्वं वा अत्तीति........सर्वस्येतस्यात्ता भवति सर्वमस्यान्नं भवति । (*sa yadyadevāsṛjata tattadattumadhriyata sarvaṃ vā attīti........sarvasyetasyāttā bhavati sarvamasyānnaṃ bhavati |*) (Jya. U. 1-2-5) **NOTE**

Prajāpati began to devour again all that he himself had produced. The whole universe is food to him. As is described in such sentences, so it is stated in the present context also. The time when he offered the world to himself in the form of an oblation is the time of dissolution. At this time death covers the whole universe. Then Īśvara is without a second. He wishes to have a second again. The time of the contraction of the world too is not eternal; it must expand. For the world covered by death to expand, the resolve of Īśvara is absolutely necessary. As soon as this resolve is made, expansion follows again. By the words आशिषा द्रविण मिच्छमानः (*āśiṣā draviṇa micchamānaḥ*) this resolve of Īśvara is also indicated. The expansion of the world covered by death and its stages are described in one place in one way and in another place in another way. In the Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad there is the following account.

नैवेह किंचनाग्र आसीन्मृत्यु नैवेदमावृतमासीत् । अशनाययाशनाया हि मृत्युस्तन्मनोऽकुरुतात्मन्वीस्यामिति । सोऽर्चन्नचरत्तस्यार्चत आपोऽजायंतार्चते वै कमभूदिति............ ॥ (*naiveha kiṃcanāgra āsīnmṛtyu naivedamāvṛtamāsīt | aśanāyayāśanāyā hi mṛtyustanmano 'kurutātmanvīsyāmiti | so 'rcannacarattasyārcata āpo 'jāyaṃtārcate vai kamabhūditi............ ‖*) (Bṛ. U. 1-2-1)

### Printed p. 184

At the beginning everything was empty. Everything was covered by death alone. This death was a hunger-like desire. Then it wished to become conscious. When it moved with this wish, water came into being. It became a venerable thing.

सोऽकामयत द्वितीयो म आत्मा जायेतेति स मनसा वाचं मिथुनं समभवदशनाया मृत्युस्तद्यद्रेत आसीत्स संवत्सरोऽभवत् । न ह पुरा ततः संवत्सर आस । तमेतावंतं कालमबिभः । यावत्संवत्सरस्तमेतावतः कालस्य परस्तादसृजत । तं जातमभिव्याददात्स भाणकरोऽस्य वागभवत् ॥ (*so 'kāmayata dvitīyo ma ātmā jāyeteti sa manasā vācaṃ mithunaṃ samabhavadaśanāyā mṛtyustadyadreta āsītsa saṃvatsaro 'bhavat | na ha purā tataḥ saṃvatsara āsa | tametāvaṃtaṃ kālamabibhaḥ | yāvatsaṃvatsarastametāvataḥ kālasya parastādasṛjata | taṃ jātamabhivyādadātsa bhāṇakaro 'sya vāgabhavat ‖*) (Bṛ. U. 1-2-4)

That death wished that a second form should be produced from it. Then that same death brought speech and mind together. The seed born from this union of speech and mind took the form of the year. Before this there was no principle called the year. Then for the length of a year it bore it. When the year was produced, death opened its mouth to devour it. Then the year, in the form of an infant, made the sound भाण् (*bhāṇ*). This became speech. (स यद्यदेवासृजत तत्तदत्तुमध्रियत (*sa yadyadevāsṛjata tattadattumadhriyata*): it began to devour all that it had produced. The whole world produced became food for it.)

स तया वाचा तेनात्मनेदं सर्वमसृजत (*sa tayā vācā tenātmanedaṃ sarvamasṛjata*) (Bṛ. U. 1-2-5)

Thus the nature of the expansion of the world covered by death is described: all the principles of the world were produced by this union of speech and mind. That death too which pervades the world has been created by the supreme Brahman himself. How is that? Thus:

प्रजापतिः प्रजा असृजत । स ऊर्ध्वोभ्य एव प्राणेभ्यो देवानसृजत । येऽर्वांचः प्राणास्तेभ्यो मर्त्याः प्रजा अथोर्ध्वमेव मृत्युं प्रजाभ्योत्तारमसृजत । (*prajāpatiḥ prajā asṛjata | sa ūrdhvobhya eva prāṇebhyo devānasṛjata | ye 'rvāṃcaḥ prāṇāstebhyo martyāḥ prajā athordhvameva mṛtyuṃ prajābhyottāramasṛjata |*) (Śa. Brā. 10-1-3-1)

Prajāpati created beings. From the upper breaths he created the gods, and from the lower breaths mortal men. Then, in order to devour the beings created by himself, he at once produced death as well. Thus creation and dissolution go on alternately and without a break. This very dissolution is described in the present context as a sacrifice in the form of an oblation. Sāyaṇa, explaining this very thing, says that Viśvakarman, the supreme Lord, offering these worlds

### Printed p. 185

in the form of an oblation at the time of dissolution, withdrawing into himself these seven worlds beginning with the earth, is the seer, who sees beyond the senses, the Hotṛ, the doer of the offering in the form of dissolution. Viśvakarman, the supreme Lord, offering into himself this whole world created by himself (the seven worlds beginning with the earth) in the form of an oblation, is the doer of the offering in the form of dissolution, omniscient and without a second: this is the purport of the sentence, he has stated.

The Lord, who is without a second, cannot remain so for ever. In order to have a second, desire arises in him as well. That very thing is stated in the present Ṛk: आशिषा द्रविणमिच्छमानः । पुनः पुनः सिसृक्षया जगद्योगमाकांक्षमाणः । (*āśiṣā draviṇamicchamānaḥ | punaḥ punaḥ sisṛkṣayā jagadyogamākāṃkṣamāṇaḥ |*). It is stated that he again wishes the enjoyment of the world and wishes to begin creation, and that he also enters into all the things created by himself (प्रथममृच्छत् (*prathamamṛcchat*); अवरान् आविवेश (*avarān āviveśa*)).

The order of the desire (kāma) by which Īśvara wished to become one with a second, and the order in which he spread out the world and, expanding together with it, became the inner controller of all, are told in the Taittirīya Āraṇyaka as follows.

आपो ना इदमासन्स्तिलमेव । स प्रजापतिरेकः पुष्करपर्णॆ समभवत् । तस्यांतर्मनसि कामस्समवर्तत । इदग्ं सृजेय मिति । तस्माद्यत्पुरुषो मनसाभिगच्छति । तद्वाचा वदति । तत्कर्मणा करोति । तदेषाभ्यनूक्ता । (*āpo nā idamāsanstilameva | sa prajāpatirekaḥ puṣkaraparṇè samabhavat | tasyāṃtarmanasi kāmassamavartata | idagṃ sṛjeya miti | tasmādyatpuruṣo manasābhigacchati | tadvācā vadati | tatkarmaṇā karoti | tadeṣābhyanūktā |*) (Tai. Ā. 1-23-1)

Before creation the whole universe was covered with water. On that water, on a lotus leaf, Prajāpati stood alone. Then in his mind arose the desire (kāma) to create the universe. This followed the worldly order. When desire (wish) arises in a man, he expresses that wish in speech, and he brings that wish to its end in his action. Prajāpati too followed this same worldly order.

कामस्तदग्रे समवर्तताधि । मनसो रेतः प्रथमं यदासीत् । सतो बंधुमसति निरविंदन् । हृदि प्रतीष्या कवयो मनीषेति । उपैनं तदुप. नमति । यत्कामो भवति । य एवं वेद । (*kāmastadagre samavartatādhi | manaso retaḥ prathamaṃ yadāsīt | sato baṃdhumasati niraviṃdan | hṛdi pratīṣyā kavayo manīṣeti | upainaṃ tadupa. namati | yatkāmo bhavati | ya evaṃ veda |*) (Śrī. Ā. 1-23-2) **NOTE**

To Prajāpati first arose desire (the wish for the creation of the world). It became the first seed of the mind. The wise poets (the breaths of Prajāpati) tried to understand the secret of this seed by their inner power

### Printed p. 186

and understood that this seed, in the form of desire, is the binding material between being and non-being. In this way all who wish to know go to those who know such a secret of the principle and take refuge with them.

स तपोऽतप्यत । स तपस्तप्त्वा । शरीरमधूनुत । तस्य यन्मार्गं समासीत् (*sa tapo 'tapyata | sa tapastaptvā | śarīramadhūnuta | tasya yanmārgaṃ samāsīt*)

As soon as that wish arose, Prajāpati practised deep austerity. Afterwards he shook his body.

ततोऽरुणाः केतवो वातरशना ऋषय उदतिष्ठन् । ये नखाः ते वैखानसाः । ये वालाः । ते वालखिल्याः । यो रसः । सोऽपां । अंतरतः कूर्मंभूतग्ं सर्पंतं । (*tato 'ruṇāḥ ketavo vātaraśanā ṛṣaya udatiṣṭhan | ye nakhāḥ te vaikhānasāḥ | ye vālāḥ | te vālakhilyāḥ | yo rasaḥ | so 'pāṃ | aṃtarataḥ kūrmaṃbhūtagṃ sarpaṃtaṃ |*) (Trai. Ā. 1-23-2,3) **NOTE**

From this shaken body of Prajāpati arose three groups of seers named Aruṇa, Ketu and Vātaraśana. His nails became known as the Vaikhānasa seers, and his hairs as the Vālakhilyas. The essence of his body took the form of a tortoise moving within the water.

तमब्रवीत् । मम वै त्वङ्मागांसा । समभूत् । नेत्यब्रवीत् । पूर्वमेवाहमिहासमिति । तत्पुरुषस्य पुरुषत्वं । स सहस्रशीर्षा पुरुषः । सहस्राक्षस्सहस्रपात् । भूत्वो दतिष्ठत् । (*tamabravīt | mama vai tvaṅmāgāṃsā | samabhūt | netyabravīt | pūrvamevāhamihāsamiti | tatpuruṣasya puruṣatvaṃ | sa sahasraśīrṣā puruṣaḥ | sahasrākṣassahasrapāt | bhūtvo datiṣṭhat |*) (Tai. Ā. 1-23.3,4)

Prajāpati said to that tortoise, which moved within the water: "You have arisen from my skin and my flesh." The tortoise answered: "I have not newly arisen; I have been here from time without beginning." Because it had existed from before, it became named Puruṣa. Then a Puruṣa with a thousand heads, a thousand eyes and a thousand feet appeared and stood up.

तमब्रवीत् । त्वं वै पूर्वग्ं समभूः । त्वमिदं पूर्वः कुरुष्टेति । स इत आदायापः । अंजलिना पुरस्तादुपादधात् । एवाह्येवेति । तत आदित्य उदितिष्ठत् । सा प्राची दिक् । अथारुणः केतुर्दक्षिणत उपादधात् । एवाह्यग्न इति । ततो ना अग्नि रुदतिष्ठत् । सा दक्षिणा दिक् । अथारुणः केतुः पश्चादुपादधात् । एवा हि वायो इति । ततो वायुरुदतिष्ठत् । सा प्रतीची दिक् । अथारुणः । केतुरुत्तरत उपाददात् । एवाहींद्रेति । ततो ना इंद्र उदतिष्ठत् । सोदीची दिक् । अथारुणः केतुर्मध्य उपादधात् । एवा हि पूषन्निति । ततो वै पूषोदतिष्ठत् । सेयं दिक् । अथारुणः केतुरुपरिष्टादुपादधात् । एवा हि देवा इति । (*tamabravīt | tvaṃ vai pūrvagṃ samabhūḥ | tvamidaṃ pūrvaḥ kuruṣṭeti | sa ita ādāyāpaḥ | aṃjalinā purastādupādadhāt | evāhyeveti | tata āditya uditiṣṭhat | sā prācī dik | athāruṇaḥ keturdakṣiṇata upādadhāt | evāhyagna iti | tato nā agni rudatiṣṭhat | sā dakṣiṇā dik | athāruṇaḥ ketuḥ paścādupādadhāt | evā hi vāyo iti | tato vāyurudatiṣṭhat | sā pratīcī dik | athāruṇaḥ | keturuttarata upādadāt | evāhīṃdreti | tato nā iṃdra udatiṣṭhat | sodīcī dik | athāruṇaḥ keturmadhya upādadhāt | evā hi pūṣanniti | tato vai pūṣodatiṣṭhat | seyaṃ dik | athāruṇaḥ keturupariṣṭādupādadhāt | evā hi devā iti |*)

### Printed p. 187

ततो देव- मनुष्याः पितरः । गंधर्वाप्सरसश्चोदतिष्ठन् । सोध्वा दिक् । या विपुषो विपरापतन् । ताभ्योऽसुरा रक्षागंसि पिशाचाश्चोदतिष्ठन् । तस्मात्ते पराभवन् । विपुड्भो हि ते समभवन् । तदेषाभ्यनूक्ता ॥ (*tato deva- manuṣyāḥ pitaraḥ | gaṃdharvāpsarasaścodatiṣṭhan | sodhvā dik | yā vipuṣo viparāpatan | tābhyo 'surā rakṣāgaṃsi piśācāścodatiṣṭhan | tasmātte parābhavan | vipuḍbho hi te samabhavan | tadeṣābhyanūktā ‖*) (Kai. Ā. 1-23-4, 5, 6, 7) **NOTE**

O Puruṣa, if you were produced before me, then you are lord over me too. Do you perform this work of creation. Saying this, he took water in the hollow of his hand and offered it in front. (In एवा हि देवा (*evā hi devā*) the first word एव (*eva*) means the desired object, desire, and the second एव (*eva*) is meant to be Āditya, according to the commentators.) Then, as soon as the mantra एवाह्येव (*evāhyeva*) was recited, Āditya arose. That became the eastern direction. Then Aruṇaketu took water in the hollow of his hand and placed it in the southern part. As soon as the mantra एवाह्यग्ने (*evāhyagne*) was uttered, Agni appeared. That became the southern direction. Then Aruṇaketu took water in the hollow of his hand and placed it in the western part. As soon as the mantra एवा हि वायो (*evā hi vāyo*) was recited, Vāyu arose. That became the western direction. Then Aruṇaketu took water and placed it in the northern part, and as soon as he uttered एवाहींद्र (*evāhīṃdra*), Indra appeared. That became the northern direction. Placing water in the middle, when he recited एवाहि पूषन् (*evāhi pūṣan*), Pūṣan appeared. Then placing water in the upper part, when he uttered एवा हि देवा (*evā hi devā*), from it the gods, men and fathers arose. Together with them the Gandharvas and Apsarases arose. That became the upper direction. On these occasions, from the masses of water that fell in the form of drops, the Rākṣasas and Piśācas appeared. Since they were produced from drops that were not in the form of streams, they did not attain a lasting state. They were defeated. On this matter the Śruti recites thus.

आ पो ह यद्बृहतीर्गर्भमायन् । दक्षं दधाना जनयंतीस्वयंभुं । तत इमेऽध्यसृज्यंत सर्गाः । अद्यो वा इदग्ं समभूत् । तस्मादिदग्ं सर्वं ब्रह्मस्वयंभ्विति। तस्मादिदग्ं सर्वग्ं शिथिलमिवा ध्रुवमिना भवत् । प्रजापतिर्नाव तत् । आत्मनात्मानं विधाय । तदेवानु प्राविशत् । तदेषाभ्यनुक्ता । विधाय लोकान् विधाय भूतानि । विधाय सर्वाः प्रदिशो दिशश्च । प्रजापतिः प्रथमजा ऋतस्य । आत्मनात्मानमभिसंविवेशेति। सर्वमेवेदमाप्त्वा । सर्वमवरुध्य । तदेवानुप्रविशति । य एवं वेद ॥ (*ā po ha yadbṛhatīrgarbhamāyan | dakṣaṃ dadhānā janayaṃtīsvayaṃbhuṃ | tata ime 'dhyasṛjyaṃta sargāḥ | adyo vā idagṃ samabhūt | tasmādidagṃ sarvaṃ brahmasvayaṃbhviti| tasmādidagṃ sarvagṃ śithilamivā dhruvaminā bhavat | prajāpatirnāva tat | ātmanātmānaṃ vidhāya | tadevānu prāviśat | tadeṣābhyanuktā | vidhāya lokān vidhāya bhūtāni | vidhāya sarvāḥ pradiśo diśaśca | prajāpatiḥ prathamajā ṛtasya | ātmanātmānamabhisaṃviveśeti| sarvamevedamāptvā | sarvamavarudhya | tadevānupraviśati | ya evaṃ veda ‖*) (Tai. Ā. 1-23.8,9)

The waters, of vast nature, having obtained increased power and having borne an excellent germ, when they produced Svayambhū, तमिद्गर्भं प्रथमं दध्र आपः (*tamidgarbhaṃ prathamaṃ dadhra āpaḥ*) यत्र देवाः (*yatra devāḥ*)

### Printed p. 188

समगच्छंत विश्वे (*samagacchaṃta viśve*) (10-82-6): all creation came into manifestation from there. Because all this was produced from one single source, all this is of the form of Brahman named Svayambhū. In the world, water is loose and unstable; therefore all beings produced from it became loose and unstable. Prajāpati too took this same form. He entered all these worlds produced from himself: अवरा आविवेश (*avarā āviveśa*) (1.81.1) **NOTE**. For this very reason the Śruti sentence begins विधाय लोकान् (*vidhāya lokān*) and so on. Its purport is that Prajāpati created all the worlds and all the quarters and intermediate quarters. This Prajāpati, born of water, entered his own body in the form of the world and became one with it.

The primal principle that was at first without a second, and then, wishing to have a second, आशिषा द्रविणमिच्छमानः (*āśiṣā draviṇamicchamānaḥ*) (1-81-1), created the world full of variety and entered into it himself, is designated in some places as Prajāpati, in some places as simply Ātman (Paramātman), in other places as Hiraṇyagarbha, and in others as Viśvakarman. But the desire for creation and the order of creation are the same in the main matters; only in some matters do they differ.

The principle named Viśvakarman, which, having accomplished the offering in the form of dissolution, is described in the present Ṛk as being without a second after the dissolution, its being without a second, its desire in creation, the creation that is the fruit of its austerity, and its identity with the created world: all this is praised in the Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad in the following way. The tortoise that existed before creation and that was called Puruṣa because of its priority, as praised in the passages of the Taittirīya Āraṇyaka quoted above: the same idea is in this account also (Tai. Ā. 1-23).

आत्मैवेदमग्र आसीत्पुरुषविधः सोऽनुवीक्ष्य नान्यदात्मनोऽपश्यत्सोऽह मस्मीत्यग्रे व्याहरत्ततोऽहं नामाभवत्तस्मादष्टेतर्हा मंत्रितोऽहमयमित्येवाग्र उक्त्वाथान्यन्नाम प्रब्रूते यदस्य भवति स यत्तूर्वोऽस्मात्सर्वस्मात्सर्वान्पाप्मन औषत्तस्मात्पुरुष ओषति ह वै स तं योऽस्मात्तूर्वो बुभूशति य एवं वेद । (*ātmaivedamagra āsītpuruṣavidhaḥ so 'nuvīkṣya nānyadātmano 'paśyatso 'ha masmītyagre vyāharattato 'haṃ nāmābhavattasmādaṣṭetarhā maṃtrito 'hamayamityevāgra uktvāthānyannāma prabrūte yadasya bhavati sa yattūrvo 'smātsarvasmātsarvānpāpmana auṣattasmātpuruṣa oṣati ha vai sa taṃ yo 'smāttūrvo bubhūśati ya evaṃ veda |*) (Bṛ. U. 1-4-1)

In the world, before creation, there was only the principle of Ātman, of the nature of pure consciousness. When it looked around, no other principle than itself was seen. Then the existence of its own self, denoted by the word "I", became evident to it: "I alone am." This Paramātman, denoted by the word "I", burnt up all

### Printed p. 189

evils (पाप्मानं औषत् (*pāpmānaṃ auṣat*): पूर्वमौषदिति पुरुषः (*pūrvamauṣaditi puruṣaḥ*): Śa. Bhā. **NOTE**); therefore it came to be denoted by the word Puruṣa.

सोऽबिभेत्तस्मादेकाकी बिभेति स हायमीक्षां चक्रे यन्मदन्यन्नास्ति कस्मान्नु बिभेमीति तत एवास्य भयं वीयाय कस्माद्भेष्यदद्वितीयाद्वै भयं भवति । (*so 'bibhettasmādekākī bibheti sa hāyamīkṣāṃ cakre yanmadanyannāsti kasmānnu bibhemīti tata evāsya bhayaṃ vīyāya kasmādbheṣyadadvitīyādvai bhayaṃ bhavati |*) (Bṛ. U. 1-4-2)

For that which has nothing with it and is alone, fear is certain. Therefore this Ātman, being alone, thought: there is no other thing that causes me fear; seeing what should I be afraid? But though fear was removed, room was made for another kind of worry.

स वै नैव रेमे । तस्मादेकाकी न रमते, स द्वितीयमैच्छत् । स हैतावानास यथा स्त्रीपुमांसौ संपरिष्वक्तौ स इयमेवात्मानं द्वेधापातयत्ततः पतिश्च पत्निचाभवतः........ ...मनुष्या अजायंत ॥ (*sa vai naiva reme | tasmādekākī na ramate, sa dvitīyamaicchat | sa haitāvānāsa yathā strīpumāṃsau saṃpariṣvaktau sa iyamevātmānaṃ dvedhāpātayattataḥ patiśca patnicābhavataḥ........ ...manuṣyā ajāyaṃta ‖*) (Bṛ. U. 1-4-3)

If one is alone, with no one with him, there is no joy in it. In the same way this Ātman too, having no one with it, had no joy. Wishing to have a person with it, it took the expansion in the form of the union of woman and man. Then, in that measure, this Ātman was divided into two parts. The same took the two forms of woman and man (wife and husband). From this the whole host of beings, men and the rest, was produced.

..................एवमेव यदिदं किंच मिथुनमा पिपीलिकाभ्यस्तत्सर्वम सृजत ॥ (*..................evameva yadidaṃ kiṃca mithunamā pipīlikābhyastatsarvama sṛjata ‖*) (Bṛ. U. 1-1-4) **NOTE**

In this way it created pairs in the form of woman and man, beginning with the human couple and going down to all living creatures such as ants.

सोऽवेदहं वाव सृष्टिरस्म्यहं हीदं सर्वमसृक्षीति ततः सृष्टिरभवत्सृष्टां हास्यैतस्यां भवति य एवं वेद ॥ (*so 'vedahaṃ vāva sṛṣṭirasmyahaṃ hīdaṃ sarvamasṛkṣīti tataḥ sṛṣṭirabhavatsṛṣṭāṃ hāsyaitasyāṃ bhavati ya evaṃ veda ‖*) (Bṛ. U. 1.4-5)

"I myself have taken these many forms by my own creation." This became evident to this Ātman. The knowledge also arose that all the beings that were created are only modifications of it. He who knows this obtains this power. (He sees his own reflection in the thing created by himself.) In such descriptions the special acts of Viśvakarman set forth in the present Ṛk, अशिषा इच्छमानः (*aśiṣā icchamānaḥ*) and अवरान् आ विवेश (*avarān ā viveśa*), are described.

### Printed p. 190

प्रजापतिर्ह वा इदमग्र एक एवास । स ऐक्षत कथं नु प्रजायेयेति । सोऽश्राम्यत्स तपोऽतप्यत सोऽग्निमेव मुखाज्जनयांचक्रे । तद्यदेनं मुखादजनयत तस्मादन्नादोऽग्निः । (*prajāpatirha vā idamagra eka evāsa | sa aikṣata kathaṃ nu prajāyeyeti | so 'śrāmyatsa tapo 'tapyata so 'gnimeva mukhājjanayāṃcakre | tadyadenaṃ mukhādajanayata tasmādannādo 'gniḥ |*) (Śa. Brā. 2-2-3-1)

In some places the episode is also described in which Prajāpati, who was without a second and wished, in order to have a second, to create the world, became afraid of the very principle created by himself. For example: before creation Prajāpati was alone. Then, wishing to increase himself by his own creation, he practised austerity. As the fruit of that austerity Agni was produced from his mouth. Because he was produced in the mouth, Agni became an eater of food.

अथैनमग्निर्व्यात्तेनोप पर्याववर्त । तस्य भीतस्य स्वो महिमापचक्राम । ..................स हुत्वा प्रजापतिः । प्र चाजायतात्सत्यतश्चाग्नेरात्मान. मत्तायत स यो ह्येवं विद्वानग्निहोत्रं जुहोति................... अग्नेर्मृत्योरात्मानं त्रायते ॥ (*athainamagnirvyāttenopa paryāvavarta | tasya bhītasya svo mahimāpacakrāma | ..................sa hutvā prajāpatiḥ | pra cājāyatātsatyataścāgnerātmāna. mattāyata sa yo hyevaṃ vidvānagnihotraṃ juhoti................... agnermṛtyorātmānaṃ trāyate ‖*) (Śa. Brā. 2-2-4:5)

For Agni, the eater of food, nothing other than Prajāpati was found to eat. Then he opened his mouth and tried to swallow Prajāpati himself. Prajāpati, frightened at this, at once produced plants and the like and offered them to Agni in the form of an oblation. An episode in the form of a narrative is also told, to the effect that he who knows this principle performs the Agnihotra and, as Prajāpati escaped from Agni, who is in the form of death, escapes likewise. This context shows the greatness of the Agnihotra.

The details set forth in the present Ṛk, ऋषिर्होता न्यसीदत् पिता नः (*ṛṣirhotā nyasīdat pitā naḥ*) and so on, are told at length in the Aitareya Upaniṣad.

ओं आत्मा वा इदमेक एवाग्र आसीत् । नान्यत्किंचन मिषत् । स ईक्षत लोकान्नु सृजा इति ॥ (*oṃ ātmā vā idameka evāgra āsīt | nānyatkiṃcana miṣat | sa īkṣata lokānnu sṛjā iti ‖*) (I. U. 1-1) **NOTE**

In the beginning there was only the principle of Ātman, of pure form. No other principle than this existed. Then this universal Self wished: "I shall create a world endowed with name and form."

स इमाल्लोकानसृजत । अंभो मरीचीर्मरमापोऽदोंभः परेण दिवं द्यौ प्रतिष्ठांतरिक्षं मरीचयः पृथिवी मरो या अधस्तात्ता आपः (*sa imāllokānasṛjata | aṃbho marīcīrmaramāpo 'doṃbhaḥ pareṇa divaṃ dyau pratiṣṭhāṃtarikṣaṃ marīcayaḥ pṛthivī maro yā adhastāttā āpaḥ*) (I. U. 1-2)

### Printed p. 191

Then, according to that wish, he created these worlds. Together with them he also produced radiance, water and death. The water created by this is above the heaven-world; the heaven-world itself is its support. The rays of radiance are the atmosphere. The earth is of the form of death. What is below it is also water.

स ईक्षतेमे नु लोका लोकपालान्नु सृजा इति । सोऽद्भ्य एव पुरुषं समुद्धृत्यामूर्छयत् ॥ (*sa īkṣateme nu lokā lokapālānnu sṛjā iti | so 'dbhya eva puruṣaṃ samuddhṛtyāmūrchayat ‖*) (I. U. 1-3)

"These three worlds have now been created. Now I must create the guardians who protect these worlds." So wishing, he created a human form out of the very water created by himself and gave it a shape.

ता एता देवताः सृष्टा अस्मिन्महत्यर्णवे प्रापतन् । तमशनापिपासाभ्यामन्व वार्जत् । ता एन मब्रुवन्नायतनं नः प्रजानीहि यस्मिन्प्रतिष्ठिता अन्नमदामेति । (*tā etā devatāḥ sṛṣṭā asminmahatyarṇave prāpatan | tamaśanāpipāsābhyāmanva vārjat | tā ena mabruvannāyatanaṃ naḥ prajānīhi yasminpratiṣṭhitā annamadāmeti |*) (I. U. 2-8)

All the deities created by this Ātman fell into the great ocean. Then hunger overcame them. The deities begged this Ātman: "Provide us a dwelling. Provide us the convenience of living there happily and eating food."

तमशनापिपासे अब्रूतामावाभ्यामभिप्रजानीहीति । ते अब्रवीदेतास्वेव वां देवतास्वाभजाम्येतासु भागिन्यौ करोमीति । तस्माद्यस्यैकस्यॆ च देवताय्कॆ हविर्गृह्यते भागिन्यावेवास्यामशनापिपासे भवतः ॥ (*tamaśanāpipāse abrūtāmāvābhyāmabhiprajānīhīti | te abravīdetāsveva vāṃ devatāsvābhajāmyetāsu bhāginyau karomīti | tasmādyasyaikasyè ca devatāykè havirgṛhyate bhāginyāvevāsyāmaśanāpipāse bhavataḥ ‖*) (I. U. 9-5)

In order to provide food for the deities as they prayed, hunger and thirst, both of them, approached this Paramātman and prayed: "Provide a good dwelling for us also." Then the Ātman said to them: "I shall provide you also a dwelling among these very deities. Whatever oblation is offered to the deities, hunger and thirst have a share in it."

### Printed p. 192

The chief purport of this passage is this: where there is plenty of food, there hunger and thirst exist. Where there is creation, there is death. Where there is expansion and pervasion of the world, there is also contraction. Plenty and want, creation and dissolution, expansion and contraction are pairs that are eternally present in the sport of Īśvara. All these are subject to Īśvara. In sum, the threefold power of Īśvara, the cause of creation, preservation and dissolution, which is described in the Ṛk अस्य वामस्य पलितस्य होतुः (*asya vāmasya palitasya hotuḥ*) (1-164-1), is described in the present Ṛk also as the same triad of powers. Describing this very thing, the Taittirīya Upaniṣad says:

सोऽकामयत । बहु स्यां प्रजायेयेति । स तपोऽतप्यत । स तपस्तप्त्वा । इदग्ं सर्वमसृजत । यदिदं किंच । तत्सृष्ट्वा । तदेवानु प्राविशत् । तदनु प्रविश्य । सच्च त्यच्चाभवत् । निरुक्तं चानिरुक्तं च । निलयनं चानिलयनं च। विज्ञानं चाविज्ञानं च। सत्यं चानृतं च सत्यमभवत्। यदिदं किंच । तत्सत्यमित्याचक्षते । तदस्येष श्लोको भवति । (*so 'kāmayata | bahu syāṃ prajāyeyeti | sa tapo 'tapyata | sa tapastaptvā | idagṃ sarvamasṛjata | yadidaṃ kiṃca | tatsṛṣṭvā | tadevānu prāviśat | tadanu praviśya | sacca tyaccābhavat | niruktaṃ cāniruktaṃ ca | nilayanaṃ cānilayanaṃ ca| vijñānaṃ cāvijñānaṃ ca| satyaṃ cānṛtaṃ ca satyamabhavat| yadidaṃ kiṃca | tatsatyamityācakṣate | tadasyeṣa śloko bhavati |*) (Tai. U. 2-6) **NOTE**

"I, who am without a second, must take many forms. Therefore, though unborn, I must come into being in the form of the world." So the Paramātman wished and practised austerity. As its fruit he created all this world. Afterwards he entered all the beings created by himself. Afterwards, though he was said to be the unlimited principle (thing), he became limited (by having entered the bodies of living beings). That is, the Ātman, who was one thing, appeared in many forms. Though endowed with the qualities of being immeasurable and endless, he became subject to measure. Though indescribable, he became subject to description. Though without support, he became subject to support or refuge. Though without consciousness, he became possessed of consciousness (became endowed with the sense of "I"). Though of the nature of truth, he became of the form of untruth; that is, though of eternal nature, he became a non-eternal thing that meets dissolution. Though beyond everything, he had entered all living beings. Such explanations are given. How such mutually opposed natures arise is this: the Ātman (Paramātman), who is unmanifest, without attributes, all-pervading and eternal, is one kind of thing. After this Paramātman has entered the moving and unmoving things created by himself, the qualities he had, unmanifestness, absence of attributes, all-pervasiveness and eternity, disappear for the time being, and in their place the qualities found in ordinary beings, limitation, non-eternity and so on, become visible: this is the purport.

### Printed p. 193

Viśvakarman, son of Bhuvana, is the seer of this sūkta and of the next. The deity too is named Viśvakarman. But whether these two are one and the same person or different persons is not clear. Considering the profound Vedāntic principles in these sūktas, it would be reasonable to say that both are the same person. In connection with this Ṛk Sāyaṇa has quoted a passage of the Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa:

ब्रह्म वै स्वयंभु तपोऽतप्यत । तदैक्षत न वै तपस्यानंत्यमस्ति हंताहं भूतेष्वात्मानं जुहवानि भूतानि चात्मनीति तत्सर्वेषु भूतेश्वात्मानं हुत्वा (*brahma vai svayaṃbhu tapo 'tapyata | tadaikṣata na vai tapasyānaṃtyamasti haṃtāhaṃ bhūteṣvātmānaṃ juhavāni bhūtāni cātmanīti tatsarveṣu bhūteśvātmānaṃ hutvā*) (Śa. Brā. 13-7-1)

The purport of this Brāhmaṇa sentence is: Brahmā, called Svayambhū (that is, Prajāpati), practised austerity in the beginning. This austerity has neither end nor beginning; however much one does, it cannot be finished. It is endless. By finishing this austerity the desired object cannot be obtained. Therefore, "I shall perform the sacrifice called Sarvamedha. The speciality of this sacrifice is to offer oneself as an oblation in all beings and to offer all the classes of beings in oneself." So thinking, he did accordingly.

To make this matter still clearer, the commentator has explained here the Nirukta of Yāska.

विश्वकर्मा सर्वमेधे सर्वाणि भूतानि जुहवांचकार स आत्मानमप्यंततो जुहवांचकार । तदभिवादिन्येषर्गभवति य इमा विश्वा भुवनानि जुह्वदिति ॥ (*viśvakarmā sarvamedhe sarvāṇi bhūtāni juhavāṃcakāra sa ātmānamapyaṃtato juhavāṃcakāra | tadabhivādinyeṣargabhavati ya imā viśvā bhuvanāni juhvaditi ‖*) (Ni. 10-26)

The seer named Viśvakarman undertook the sacrifice called Sarvamedha, offered all beings in it, and at last offered himself in the fire as an oblation. This very matter is propounded in the Ṛk य इमा विश्वा (*ya imā viśvā*): such is the purport of the explanation.

In this sūkta there is a description of the creation, preservation and dissolution carried out by the Lord of all. In this first Ṛk at the beginning there is the description of dissolution. Since creation, preservation and dissolution go on without a break, one after another in alternation, one cannot say which of them is first and which last. Any of them may be mentioned first. Therefore here the matter of dissolution has been taken up first.

य इमा विश्वा भुवनानि जुह्वत् .... स अवरान् आ विवेश (*ya imā viśvā bhuvanāni juhvat .... sa avarān ā viveśa*): it was stated earlier that the deity of this sūkta is Viśvakarman. Who is this Viśvakarman? What is his nature? It is necessary to examine this closely. As Yāska says, विश्वकर्माविश्वस्य कर्ता (*viśvakarmāviśvasya kartā*), that is, the meaning may be given as: he who has made the universe itself. By universe is meant everything, that is, the whole host of worlds and cosmic eggs, beginning with the world in which we live. This universe

### Printed p. 194

is endless, there is no limit to it. He who has created all this by his wonderful great power is called Viśvakarman. He may be called by any word, such as Parabrahman, Sarveśvara, Paramātmā and so on. For the power to create a universe does not belong to the other ordinary deities. Since it is the Supreme Lord, who is greater than all and all-pervading, who is able to do this work, there is no doubt at all that this Parameśvara is Viśvakarman. It is from this point of view that this sūkta and the following sūktas must be examined and their meaning fitted together. Now, in the sentence य इमा विश्वा भुवनानि जुह्वत् ऋषिर्होता (*ya imā viśvā bhuvanāni juhvat ṛṣirhotā*) it is said that this Sarveśvara made a sacrifice of this whole universe, that is, of the hosts of cosmic eggs, or offered it as an oblation. जुह्वत् (*juhvat*), "made a sacrifice", means here "destroyed". Just as the ghee and other substances offered in the fire merge into the fire itself and are no longer to be found as they were, so the thing that has been offered does not come back. That is, that thing loses its former state and attains a different state. This does not mean that complete destruction takes place; the thing attains an altered condition. That is, the ghee offered in the fire is not destroyed. It is transformed into the form of brilliance. The ghee that was visible to our eyes attains a changed state that is called light or heat. In the same way, when it is said that Viśvakarman sacrificed this universe, that is, the many worlds and all the moving and unmoving things in them, all those worlds are destroyed, that is, they no longer remain in the state in which they are now, but attain another form. That is, in a subtle form they become one with the Supreme Self itself. For all the worlds that arise from the Supreme Self are dissolved in the Supreme Self itself. At the time of creation they arise again from that same Supreme Self.

नि असीदत् पिता नः (*ni asīdat pitā naḥ*)—the words पिता नः (*pitā naḥ*) indicate Viśvakarman, or Sarveśvara, who is our protector and the cause of our origin. After he had sacrificed all the worlds, as said in the sentence above, that is, after he had dissolved (destroyed) them, that Sarveśvara नि असीदत् (*ni asīdat*) remained hidden, himself unperceived by anyone. That is, just as he offered the worlds, he also offered himself. Giving up visibility to the senses, he remained formless, in a subtle state. At that time there were no distinctions of any kind, such as who the Supreme Self is and which are the worlds. Everything was without name and form, and only the Brahman-nature existed. This state of Parabrahman may be called non-being (asat). Non-being does not mean an empty void in which there is nothing; it is the subtle state of the Self (the Supreme Self), which is without name and form, that is, changeless, and all-pervading. Again, at the time of creation, from this changeless Brahman called non-being, the worlds provided with name and form, called being (sat), come into manifestation.

### Printed p. 195

Here the commentator has said: आत्मकृतेन कर्मणा देहोत्पत्तेः । न चैकस्य जन्यजनकभावो विरुध्यते तपोबलेन शरीरद्वयस्वीकारात् । (*ātmakṛtena karmaṇā dehotpatteḥ | na caikasya janyajanakabhāvo virudhyate tapobalena śarīradvayasvīkārāt |*). That is, according to the actions that each performs, the bodies of the respective beings arise. Here Sarveśvara creates, and he himself destroys. And how can he himself be both the cause of the origin of a thing and the thing that has originated? Is this not mutually contradictory? To this the answer is given that by the power of austerity the relation of originator and originated is possible, and that it is possible to take two bodies at the same time by the power of austerity. In the Chāndogya Upaniṣad there is the statement स एकधा भवति (*sa ekadhā bhavati*) (Chā. U. 7-26-2): that Supreme Self, who was in many kinds of forms, becomes one who has a single form. And in the Taittirīya Upaniṣad there is the utterance of the Supreme Self बहु स्यां प्रजायेयेति (*bahu syāṃ prajāyeyeti*): "I wish to become many forms." That is, the meaning contained in these Upaniṣadic sentences is that at the time of creation that Supreme Self itself is in many kinds of forms, and at the time of dissolution becomes one. For all the moving and unmoving things and beings seen in the world are forms of Parabrahman. Apart from Brahman there is no other thing. Therefore that Brahman itself exists in many forms at the time of creation and in a single form at the time of dissolution. This Brahman in the state of dissolution is described by the sentence नि आसीदत् पिता नः (*ni āsīdat pitā naḥ*). In this sentence there is a description of the state that exists after the dissolution and before creation. The state that existed at that time is well described by the 129th sūkta of the tenth maṇḍala of the Ṛg-veda-saṃhitā.

नासदासीन्नो सदासीत्तदानीं नासीद्रजो नो व्योमा परो यत् । किमावरीवः कुह कस्य शर्मन्नंभः किमासीद्गहनं गभीरं । न मृत्युरासीदमृतं न तर्हि न रात्रा अह्न आसीत्पकेतः । आनीदवातं स्वधया तदेकं तस्माद्दान्यन्न परः किंचनास । तम आसीत्तमसा गूळ्वमग्रेऽप्रकेतं सलिलं सर्वमा इदं ॥ (*nāsadāsīnno sadāsīttadānīṃ nāsīdrajo no vyomā paro yat | kimāvarīvaḥ kuha kasya śarmannaṃbhaḥ kimāsīdgahanaṃ gabhīraṃ | na mṛtyurāsīdamṛtaṃ na tarhi na rātrā ahna āsītpaketaḥ | ānīdavātaṃ svadhayā tadekaṃ tasmāddānyanna paraḥ kiṃcanāsa | tama āsīttamasā gūḷvamagre 'praketaṃ salilaṃ sarvamā idaṃ ‖*) (Ṛ. Saṃ. 10-129-1,2,3)

Before creation there was none of the manifest and unmanifest things that are called being and non-being. There were neither worlds nor space, nor any other thing that could be identified. There was no other thing that could cover it. There were neither pleasure and pain and the like, nor beings fit to experience them. What else was there? Was there water filling everything, boundless and like an ocean? That too was not there. Since the beings themselves were not there, death too was not there, and there was no enjoyable substance called nectar (amṛta). There was neither day nor night. When the sun and the moon themselves were not there, whence could there be night and day? Therefore there was neither night and day nor any division of time. Then what else was there? Brahman alone was there. Other than that Parabrahman substance, nothing else was there. It was not there in a manifest form.

### Printed p. 196

Just as darkness is not seen, so that Brahman was beyond the reach of sight and, like water, all-pervading. Such is the description. We shall explain this matter in detail in that context.

स आशिषा द्रविणं इच्छमानः प्रथमच्छत् अवरान् आ विवेश (*sa āśiṣā draviṇaṃ icchamānaḥ prathamacchat avarān ā viveśa*)—as explained before, is the state that existed before creation always present? If it is not, how did the creation of this world and the rest, which we now see, come about? This matter is explained by the sentence स आशिषा (*sa āśiṣā*), the second half of this Ṛk. Has it not been said before that in the state before creation only Parabrahman, in an unmanifest form, existed and that nothing else existed? As described in the śruti sentences आत्मावा इदमेक एवाग्रआसीत् । सदेव सोम्येदमग्र आसीत् । (*ātmāvā idameka evāgraāsīt | sadeva somyedamagra āsīt |*), at that time only the thing called Brahman or the Self existed; nothing else existed. After some time there arose in that Brahman the wish to create. Then that Self, or Parameśvara, desired बहु स्यां प्रजायेय (*bahu syāṃ prajāyeya*), "to take many forms", that is, he wished to create worlds and the rest in many varieties. This is explained in the Taittirīya Upaniṣad in this way:

सोकामयत । बहु स्यां प्रजायेयेति। स तपोऽतप्यत । स तपस्तप्ता । इदग्ं सर्वमसृजत । यदिदं किंच । तत्सृष्टा । तदेवानुप्राविशत् । (*sokāmayata | bahu syāṃ prajāyeyeti| sa tapo 'tapyata | sa tapastaptā | idagṃ sarvamasṛjata | yadidaṃ kiṃca | tatsṛṣṭā | tadevānuprāviśat |*) (Tai. U. 8.6)**NOTE**.

Then Parameśvara wished to attain many forms. For that he performed austerity and created this world. After creating it he entered all the moving and unmoving things and beings of the world; that is, the Self (Brahman) itself became manifest, or was transformed, in various forms: this is the meaning. He entered the things he had created, that is, he filled all things with consciousness. There is no thing in this world that is without the consciousness that is a part of the Supreme Self; it is not even possible for there to be one. When the Supreme Self itself became manifest in many forms (that is, when the world was created), it is quite natural that this whole world, which consists of parts of the Supreme Self, is endowed with the consciousness of that Self.

The main summary is this. In this Ṛk the Parameśvara (called here Viśvakarman) withdrew (destroyed) all these worlds that are visible to the eye; this is the dissolution. After that, for a long time only the Self, or the Brahman substance, in an unmanifest form existed. Nothing else existed. Then there arose in Brahman the wish to create, that is, the wish to become manifest himself in many forms. Accordingly, he created the world and the various kinds of moving and unmoving things and the beings in it, and entered them as the inner controller. This matter is set forth in this Ṛk.

### Printed p. 197

#### Ṛk 2

##### ॥ संहितापाठः ॥ (Saṃhitā-pāṭha)

> किं स्वि॑दासीदधि॒ष्ठान॑मा॒रंभ॑णं कत॒मत्स्वि॑त्क॒थासी॑त् ।  
> यतो॒ भूमिं॑ ज॒नय॑न्वि॒श्वक॑र्मा॒ वि द्यामौर्णो॑न्महि॒ना  ॒॑
> विश्वचक्षाः ॥ २ ॥

##### ॥ पदपाठः ॥ (Pada-pāṭha)

> किं । स्वित् । आसीत् । अधिस्थानं । आरंभणं । कतमत् । स्वित् । कथा । आसीत् ।  
> यतः । भूमिं । जनयन् । विश्वकर्मा । वि । द्यां । और्णोत् । महिना । विश्वचक्षाः॥

##### ॥ सायणभाष्यम् ॥ (Sāyaṇa-bhāṣya)

> वैश्वदेवस्य पशोर्वपाया अनुवाक्का किं स्विदित्येषा । सूत्रितं च । किं
> स्विदासीदधिष्ठानं यो नः पिता जनिता । आ. ३-८ । इति ॥
> पूर्वमंत्रे जगत्पलयकाले संहृत्य पश्चात्सिसृक्षायां सर्वं सृष्ट्वा तत्र
> प्रविष्ट इत्युक्तं । तत्र तस्य द्वितीयस्याधिष्ठानजगदुपादानकारणाद्यसंभवात्सृष्टिरनुषपन्नेत्याक्षिपति । लोके हि घटं चिकीर्षुः कुलालो गृहादिकं किंचित्सा नमधिष्ठाय
> मृद्रूपेण रंभद्रव्येण चक्रादिरूपैरुपकरणैर्घटं निष्पादयति । तद्वदीश्वरस्य
> जगदाश्रयद्यावापृथिव्योरुत्पादनवेलायामधिष्ठानं किं स्विदासीत् । किं नामाभूत् । न किंचिदित्यर्थः । तथा तयोरारंभणं कतमत्स्मित् । आरभ्यतेऽनेनेत्यारंभणमुपादानकारणं । तदपि कतमद्भवेत् । तदपि नेत्यर्थः । यद्यपि संभवेदारंभणं
> कथासीत् । कथमभूत् । किं स्वयं सदसद्वा भवेदित्यर्थः । उभयमपि
> नोपपद्यते । सच्चेत् अद्वैतभंगप्रसंगः । असच्चेत् सदात्मकयोर्द्यावापृथिव्यो:-
> रुपादानानर्हत्वात् । नान्यत्किंचन मिषत् । ऐ. आ. २-४.१ । इति श्रुतेश्चेत्यभिप्रायः ।
> यतो यस्मादधिष्ठानादारंभणाच्च विश्वचक्षाः सर्वद्रष्टा विश्वकर्मा परमेश्वरो
> भूमिं जनयन्वर्तते तथा द्यां दिवं व्यौर्णोत् व्यवृणोत् सृष्टवान् महिना
> स्वमहत्त्वेन । किं स्विदासीदिति ॥

##### Gist of the bhāṣya

This Ṛk, beginning किं स्वित् (*kiṃ svit*), is the anuvākyā for the offering of the omentum (vapā) of the animal belonging to the Viśvedevas. It is also stated in the sūtra: किं स्विदासीदधिष्ठानं यो नः पिता जनिता (*kiṃ svidāsīdadhiṣṭhānaṃ yo naḥ pitā janitā*) (Ā. 3-8).

Sāyaṇa says that in the previous mantra it was stated that at the time of dissolution the Lord withdrew the world, and afterwards, when the desire to create arose, created everything and entered it. Here an objection is raised: since for him, who is without a second, there could be no support, no material cause of the world and the like, creation is not possible. In the world, a potter who wishes to make a pot takes his stand on some place such as a house, and with earth as the material and with implements such as the wheel produces the pot. In the same way, for Īśvara, at the time of producing heaven and earth, which are the support of the world, what was the support? What was there? Nothing, is the meaning. Likewise, what was the आरंभणं (*āraṃbhaṇaṃ*) of those two, that is, that by which a thing is begun, the material cause? What could that be? That also was not, is the meaning. Even if it were possible, how was it? Would it itself be being or non-being? Neither is tenable. If it were being, there would be a breach of non-duality. If it were non-being, it could not be the material of heaven and earth, which consist of being. The scripture too says that nothing else existed (Ai. Ā. 2-4.1): such is the intention. Out of which support and which material the all-seeing Viśvakarman, the Supreme Lord, has been producing the earth, and likewise disclosed (made) heaven, by his own greatness: that is the question; what was it?

##### प्रतिपदार्थ (Pratipadārtha)

- यतः (*yataḥ*)—from which support and original cause
- विश्वचक्षा (*viśvacakṣā*)—all-seeing
- विश्वकर्मा (*viśvakarmā*)—the Lord of the worlds
- भूमिं (*bhūmiṃ*)—the earth
- जनयन् (*janayan*)—producing
- महिना (*mahinā*)—by his greatness
- द्यां (*dyāṃ*)—the whole heaven-world
- व्यार्णोत् (*vyārṇot*)—did he create

### Printed p. 198

- अधिष्ठानं (*adhiṣṭhānaṃ*)—the support for the creation of this world
- किं स्वित् आसीत् (*kiṃ svit āsīt*)—what was there
- आरंभणं (*āraṃbhaṇaṃ*)—the original cause for the production
- कतमत् स्वित् (*katamat svit*)—what was it
- कथा आसीत् (*kathā āsīt*)—in what manner was it

##### भावार्थ (Bhāvārtha)

The all-seeing Lord of the worlds, producing the earth and creating the whole heaven-world by his greatness: from what support and original cause did he do so? What was the support for the creation of this world? What was the original cause of its production? What was its manner? What was its nature?

##### English translation
What was the station ? what was the material ? how was (it done) ?
so that the beholder of all, Vishwakarman (was) generating and disclosed

##### विशेषविषयगळु (Special Topics)

When the offering of the omentum in the animal sacrifice made for the Viśvedevas is performed, this Ṛk beginning किं स्वित् (*kiṃ svit*) is to be recited as the anuvākyā mantra; this is set forth by the sūtra of the Āśvalāyana Śrauta Sūtra किं स्विदासीदधिष्ठानं यो नः पिता जनिता । (*kiṃ svidāsīdadhiṣṭhānaṃ yo naḥ pitā janitā |*) (Ā. 3-8).

In the previous Ṛk the nature of the dissolution of the world was stated; after some time the Supreme Lord wished to create again, created accordingly, and afterwards it was said that the Supreme Self entered all the moving and unmoving things that had been created. It has also been stated that before creation only the Brahman substance existed and that nothing else existed. It is known to everyone in worldly practice that, if a thing is to be made, three things are absolutely necessary: the place in which to make it, the original material, and the person who makes it. Since at the time of dissolution the Supreme Lord alone existed and no other thing existed, this Ṛk states how this creation could be possible.

If an example is given, it will be easier to understand the matter well. Suppose a potter makes a pot. A place is needed for making the pot. The original material needed for the pot, that is, clay, is needed. The instruments, the wheel, water and a stick, are needed. The maker, that is, the potter, who can make the pot by using all these with his intelligence, is needed. Only if all this exists is it possible to make the pot; otherwise it is not. Now at the time of dissolution the Supreme Self, who is the maker for creation, was indeed there. But where was the place? Where were the original material (Raw Materials) needed for the creation of the world, and the instruments? There was none of them. Yet the Supreme Self created the world. The seer examines this in this Ṛk by way of question and answer and explains how creation took place.

### Printed p. 199

अधिष्ठानं किं स्वित् आसीत् (*adhiṣṭhānaṃ kiṃ svit āsīt*)—at the time of the creation of the world, what was the support for the creation of this world? Since the heaven and earth that we now see, that is, the three worlds, earth, atmosphere and heaven, did not exist then, standing where, and how, did the Supreme Self make these worlds? That is, there was then no support at all for the creation of the worlds.

आरंभणं कतमत्स्वित् (*āraṃbhaṇaṃ katamatsvit*)—besides this, what was the original material (Raw Materials) needed for it at the beginning of making a thing? There was none. By original material is meant that, for making a pot, clay is the original material; for making cloth, cotton is the original material; for making vessels, metal is the original material; for making tables, chairs, cots and the like, wood (the tree) is the original material, and so on. This being so, what original material was there at that time for making this heaven and earth? There was none.

कथा आसीत् (*kathā āsīt*)—as said above, when neither place nor original material existed, the seer asks how this work of creation came about. In the Upaniṣads and other works it is said: असद्वा इदमग्र आसीत् । ततो वै सदजायत (*asadvā idamagra āsīt | tato vai sadajāyata*). That is, before creation there was non-being (asat), that which is nothing; from it being (sat), existence or the state, came into being, it is said. If a thing called being existed before creation and the world was created from it (from that original material), then the Supreme Self and being would be two things. If there are two things, where is non-duality? Therefore that is not right. Well, if non-being alone existed, how is it possible for heaven and earth, which are called being, to arise from non-being (from the void)? That is, the question naturally arises: how is it possible to create a new thing when there is nothing? According to what modern scientists say, "Nothing can be lost or nothing can be created": no thing can be completely destroyed, and likewise no new thing can be made without the help of an original material. This statement is a point to be noted especially in the principles of the Vedānta. On the basis of this very statement the nature of the creation, continuance and dissolution of the world is explained with discussion in the Vedānta principles, that is, in the Upaniṣads and the like.
